In His Own Words, a Czech Alpinist Describes How He Survived a Nightmare on Peru’s Highest Mountain

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Honza Špička on Huascarán (Photo: Courtesy of Matěj Žák)

My name is Jan “Honza” Špička. My climbing partner, Matěj Žák, and I are two alpinists, climbers, mountaineers, skiers, and endurance athletes from Nove Straseci, a small town in the Czech Republic. Although we have known each other since childhood through our families (and the local ice hockey team), our climbing paths only crossed a few years ago. Since then, we have spent three seasons together in Peru’s Cordillera Blanca and two seasons in Argentine Patagonia. We have roped up together a few times on Czech sandstone as well. We are no professionals, though; rather than extreme lines, what we have behind us are moderately difficult routes climbed in our own style: clean and fast.

Our speciality? Long days in the mountains and a fast, lightweight approach. We take nothing on an expedition that we cannot carry on our own backs.

Huascarán is the highest mountain in Peru and the highest point in the entire tropics. Its south summit reaches 6,768m and the north summit 6,655m; the two are separated by a broad col called La Garganta (“the Throat” in Spanish) at roughly 6,000m. Huascarán lies at the heart of the Cordillera Blanca, the most heavily glaciated tropical mountain range in the world, and it gave its name to the surrounding national park, a UNESCO World Heritage Site since 1985. Because the Earth bulges slightly at the equator, the gravitational acceleration on the summit of Huascarán is among the lowest anywhere on the planet’s surface; a person there measurably weighs a little less than at sea level, by a matter of grams. The summit of Huascarán is also the second most distant point on Earth from the planet’s center, right after Chimborazo.

The mountain is named after Huáscar, the Inca ruler who fought his half-brother Atahualpa for several years over control of the Inca Empire. Atahualpa eventually defeated his brother and had him executed. The American mountaineer Annie Smith Peck, then over fifty years old, climbed the north summit in 1908. The higher south summit held out until 1932, when a German–Austrian expedition made the first ascent.

Huascarán, however, also has a darker side to its history. In 1962, a huge mass of ice broke away from the mountain, and the resulting debris avalanche swept down onto the towns of Ranrahirca. Then, on May 31, 1970, an earthquake off the coast triggered a far larger and more extensive avalanche from the north summit, which within minutes buried, among other places, the entire town of Yungay. The earthquake and avalanche combined took the lives of approximately 67,000 people. It was the deadliest single avalanche disaster by death toll in recorded history.

This tragic event unfortunately also carries a strong Czechoslovak mark. It was right here, in the valley between Huascarán’s north summit and Huandoy Sur, that a Czechoslovak expedition was camped—and the whole expedition became a victim of this mountain.

If we take into account the victims of 1970 and all the climbers who have died on its slopes, it is probably the mountain responsible for more human lives than any other in modern history.

The ascent itself, via the Normal Route, is not particularly difficult, but Huascarán’s glaciers are retreating fast, and so today some routes that were relatively straightforward climbs just a generation ago involve significant objective hazards. In the six years that I have lived in Peru, I cannot remember a year when Huascarán did not claim another victim. There have even been years when it allowed no one at all to reach its summit.

The start of our trip began on Friday, July 11, in the evening in our favourite “pollo a la brasa” restaurant. Our whole group, which we called the “Cayesh crew,” met for a shared dinner to load up on a solid dose of protein before what was to be the main climbing objective of the season for all of us: Huascarán Sur. It has to be said, though, that for none of us was Huascarán the center of the universe. We all had it in “one day, maybe, I’d like to, if it comes to it…” mode, but there was certainly no obsession with this “fat one.” I already had three unsuccessful attempts behind me, but even so I wasn’t insisting on “bagging it.” Yet somehow the appetite, the motivation, the hunger for it arrived. And essentially by coincidence it arrived not only for Matěj and me but also for our friends from Peru. In the end we formed three teams of two.

I have known Saul—formally, Freddy Saul Mendoza Lizana—since 2021; he was essentially my first climbing partner here in Peru. Since then we have been through a lot together—in the mountains, on the crags, at home, and in the pub. He was like my Peruvian brother. We were so close that my wife Alča and I were already counting on a little room for Saul for when we one day return to the Czech Republic and settle down somewhere.

Agnieszka is a Polish “machine” with several 8a/5.13b sport routes to her name. She had seen plenty in the mountains, too. Definitely no lightweight.

Alejandro “Chicho” Ugarte Jordan was a professional photographer from Lima. His main interest was unique mountain images captured with his DSLR or his drone. We had known each other since 2022 through the Skiing Peru project, which we were both part of. We often spent time together with our girlfriends in Lima outside the season, too.

Artidoro “Arti” Salas Gaitan was a pure Huaracino—someone who comes from Huaraz. He was born in the town at the foot of the Cordillera Blanca and lived there his whole life. He ran the Big Mountain hostel, well known among climbers. I personally have known him since 2021, when I spent my first season in Huaraz at his hostel.

Our expedition began on the morning of Saturday, July 11, when we loaded the van with heavy packs. Under the watchful eye of—as Matěj called him—our adoptive father Paul, our friend and the owner of the cozy Cayesh Guest House bed and breakfast, we set off for our starting point, the village of Armapampa in the Yungay area. From Huaraz, it was about a two-hour drive. Besides the six of us, Alenka came along, too, having arrived from Lima that same morning, as did Míša, a Czech woman living in Nepal who had just popped over to Peru to see friends. The girls, however, were only going on a day trip to the hut and back down the next day.

On the edge of the beautiful village of Armapampa, we unloaded our gear from the van, ate the last gluten-free cinnamon rolls that Alenka baked for the trip, and shouldered our heavy packs. Matěj took a group photo and we set off towards Refugio Huascarán, our base camp. We reached the refugio at 4,650m spread out over quite a wide time range (Matěj first, at 2 hours 45 minutes, and Saul and Aga last at a little over 6 hours). Who knows where and why they lingered; apparently they were finishing off their avocados. Either way, the trail was easy and there was no rush, so everyone went at their own pace.

There was no one at the hut apart from us, so we enjoyed it immensely. The evening was a very friendly affair: dinner, beer, guitar, and singing in four languages mixed together. And, of course, planning the climb.

This is where the first, relatively pivotal moment came. Aga wasn’t feeling well—to be precise, she felt pretty crap—so she decided to walk down with the girls. That left the five of us Mohicans. Saul immediately arranged with the lads to form a three-person team. At the time, it seemed to me a good idea. Saul is the most experienced of the three, and the team would only be stronger for it. In hindsight, I blame myself that we didn’t tell him to stay with Agnieszka; Aga was his big “project.”

After breakfast at the hut, we packed our rucksacks, took a group photo, and set off. The girls headed down; the lads headed up. None of us had any idea that this would be our last photo together, and that the girls would never see Saul, Chicho, and Arti again. Just writing this sentence brings tears to my eyes.

The second day of the climb up Huascarán was easy going. A few hundred vertical meters led up to the glacier, then a few hundred more traversed over a gently angled and relatively safe glacier to the spot where Camp 1 is set, at about 5,300m. Matěj and I arrived first, and shortly after us Arti and Chicho, with Saul behind them. We pitched the tents, melted water, cooked food, took photos, rested, and fine-tuned the details for tomorrow’s summit push.

Huascarán is normally climbed via the Garganta col, where Camp 2 is placed at 5,950m, and from there climbers make for the summit. All of us, however, were heading for the El Escudo (“The Shield”) route, which is technically harder but objectively safer,and of course more fun. It is a roughly 600-meter long ice face at 60 to 70 degrees. Thanks to its shape, there are no seracs hanging above you, no cornices, and far fewer things that can come down on your head. Unfortunately, there is little room for a Camp 2 there, so the Escudo is usually climbed from Camp 1 straight to the top, with the descent via the Normal Route.

We set our departure time for 10 pm. While the sun was still up, we crawled into our sleeping bags to get at least a little sleep and recharge the batteries a bit. We all knew that brutally hard work lay ahead.

This was it: summit day. Matěj and I got up at 9:30 p.m. so that we could leave at 10. We could hear the lads sometime before 9. In the end, we left more or less together: the lads at about 10:15 pm, we a few minutes after them.

Given the profile of the route over the Escudo, the plan from the start was to climb in pairs. Later, we became a pair and a three. You cannot climb this with five people on one rope, and none of us ever suggested it. The same applies to the descent.

The first part of the route was probably the most dangerous section of the whole ascent. It was a zone of crevasses and seracs of a size you will not encounter in Europe. There were a few sections of steep ice, but it wasn’t outright hard; it was just that something could come down at any moment. This section was shared with the Normal Route, which then branched off to the left towards Camp 2, while we headed right towards El Escudo.

We reach the bergschrund in less than two hours, with the lads following an hour or so behind us. Here we re-tied onto the full length of the 60m rope and racked up all our gear. In the process, I dropped my radio somewhere into the bowels of the glacier, and Matěj set off upward. The terrain wasn’t hard, so we simul-climbed on a single rope. Matěj climbed three to four pitches this way until he ran out of ice screws. On his last two snow pickets, he built a belay and let me through. My section was more hard snow than ice:easier climbing, less fun, but with correspondingly worse protection. I had three snow pickets on my pack, but I wanted to save those for later, for the belay anchors. After climbing some six pitches on just a handful of screws, I topped out over the lip of the Escudo, where I brought up Matýsek. (Editor’s note: “Matýsek” is a Czech nickname for “Matěj.”) By then, we were at 6,300m. It was cold as hell, with the sun still nowhere in sight. I dropped a glove and started feeling properly ill from the altitude. From this moment on, I suffered like an animal. The section from the top of the Escudo to the summit was endless; it wasn’t as steep any more, but it was long, and the soft snow did nothing to help our progress.

Sometime before 9 a.m. on Monday, July 13, though, we stood on the summit of Huascarán Sur. We didn’t really feel much joy, because we knew the worst was still ahead of us: the way down. On top of that, the summit was a completely flat plateau. You could play soccer on it, only you’d have a long way to go for a stray ball. Even so, weakness came over me and I cried. It was my fourth attempt, with my partner and childhood friend, and exactly a month to the day since our dog, Pisco, was poisoned. I cried again just remembering him. But the joy was there, too, although the main part of it was supposed to come down below. And it did come. But only for a moment.

We didn’t linger on the summit. There really was nothing there. After a snack, a little tea, and the summit photo, we headed down. No one had been on the summit for about two to three weeks, so there were no tracks and no trail. Our only guide was the offline mapy.cz app, which traced the Normal Route. But it was the route that was walked an unknown number of years ago, so we treated it as a rough indication only. We re-tied into classic glacier-travel mode, roughly 15–20 meters apart, with the rest of the rope coiled around each of our bodies.

A few minutes from the summit, on the empty, white, flat plateau, I saw something that looked like the windblown hint of a trail. Before I even got there, something happened and I was falling through the air into an icy hell.

A crevasse. With no sign whatsoever of its existence, it had been waiting there silently for its victim. I remember precisely the feeling of flying into the void. It was as if I were falling for an eternity. Suddenly, I felt the rope come tight, and I knew that was Matýsek. But I keep falling. In that instant, I was certain that in a moment, Matýsek would come flying in after me and we’d both be dead.

But a miracle happened—a miracle called Matěj. The rope bit again and I stopped. He bloody well caught me. I was hanging about 12–15 meters down in a crevasse that runs 100m in each direction and could easily be 50m, 100m, or 300m deep. Who knows? Below me, I saw nothing but icy hell. The crevasse was completely covered with a cap of fine snow. Zero chance of seeing it.

To my own surprise, I didn’t panic; I knew exactly what to do. My only connection to our world was the rope I was hanging on and whatever Matěj had rigged up there to keep me in this world. I clipped a Micro Traxion onto my harness as a self-belay and climbed up the vertical ice wall towards the surface as far as I could. I felt so OK that I even took three photos inside the crevasse. When the line of the rope stopped me from climbing any farther, I swung out into space. I ascended the rest on prusiks.

In a little under half an hour, I was out, back on the surface, back in the world. Only now did I breathe out.

Matěj and I fell into each other’s arms and began to grasp what we just survived. It would have taken very little for him to come in after me, and no one would ever have found us. In hindsight, I see it as an enormous miracle and a measure of enormous skill on Matěj’s part. He saved our lives. And maybe it was at this very moment that we realized this really was no joke and that things were getting serious. We continued the descent very cautiously, and we were genuinely scared.

We reached the edge of an ice wall where the Normal Route was supposed to be, but there was nothing there—nothing at all. There was no question of searching for a way through this labyrinth. The wall looked to be about 20m high, with a ramp below it along which we hoped to traverse off left toward the Normal Route.

We rappelled off two snow pickets into a relatively safe spot surrounded by seracs. I remember looking at the leaning towers next to us and thinking that one deep breath would be enough to bring the whole lot down. Still, we felt a certain safety in our little hollow. Finally, we had a bit of time to catch our breath, eat and drink a little, and get in touch with the lads.

Before I could do that, they called me. They said they were about 200 vertical meters below the summit. But it was already late, around 12 to 1 p.m., so their plan was to traverse off towards the Normal Route and descend by it.

I told them to try to find our tracks and our rappel, and to really watch out for crevasses, because I had just fallen into one. We confirmed that everything was OK and that we would meet in the evening at Camp 1, or if need be, at base camp, depending on the time and our energy. If I had known that this was the last time I would speak to them, I would have told them so much more.

A little later, while searching for the route on the map, my phone slipped out of my hand and vanished somewhere below. But I didn’t dwell on it. I knew we had other worries. It was just a shame to lose the photos from inside the crevasse.

Another unpleasant rappel off a 25-meter serac, and we were on the slope leading towards the Garganta. By now the weather had turned and visibility had dropped to a few tens of meters. But we were already in the Garganta, at 6,000m. All we needed now was to find Camp 2, which was just a flat patch where tents stood two days ago. From there, there should be some tracks and a route down.

We wandered about a bit, but eventually we found Camp 2 and there really was a hint of tracks. Shortly before that, the summit shook off its cloud, and we saw the lads above us at about 6,300 or 6,400 m. It even looked to us as though they were closer to the Normal Route than we were. We were really relieved.

The route from Camp 2 to Camp 1 is the most dangerous crux of the climb. It is a zone of avalanches, collapsing seracs, and crevasses. Later, Matěj and I agreed that we both felt as if we were living in some kind of virtual reality, outside our bodies, just waiting for the video game to end. Maybe that was why I didn’t really register the risk.

We arrived back at Camp 1 at sunset. With our last reserves of strength, we packed up and continued down to the refugio. A hot dinner and a bed was, after all, a big draw. And it was only a little under two hours more.

We reached the hut sometime before nine in the evening, after 23 hours on our feet. We asked for something to eat and a beer, then went to sleep. We thought about the lads and somehow had no doubt that they were fine and that they were at Camp 1. After all, we saw them a short way above Camp 2.

In the morning, we woke up at the hut completely wrecked, but suddenly also happy with what we’d achieved. We expected the lads to turn up any moment, and then we’d race down into the valley. The news from the lads, however, was different.

Saul wrote that they couldn’t find the way down and had to bivouac somewhere above Camp 2. But they were OK, he said; they survived the night up there without any camping gear. They were tired, Chicho was apparently feeling ill, but all were OK. They would try to find the descent route. From then on, we were to communicate with Chicho, as Saul’s battery was dead.

We still had no idea that things were bad. But neither did the lads. Because things weren’t bad yet. We managed to download our GPS track onto a phone and send it to the lads as a mapy.cz link. Up above Camp 2, there was occasionally a signal, so we communicated with the lads via WhatsApp.

At 9:10 a.m., Chicho wrote asking us to send our route. At 9:15 a.m., we sent the mapy.cz link and it was delivered.

At 10:16 am, Chicho sent a photo and a video from where they were. They sounded fine; Camp 2 was visible below them. At 10:18 a.m., we sent them a crib sheet of roughly which way to go down, including a sketch drawn onto their photo. This was delivered. At 10:21 a.m., we asked Chicho whether he had our route on mapy.cz. This was the last message delivered to Chicho. After 10:21 am, no further messages were delivered. From then on, there was no communication from them.

Since the weather was beautiful, the lads were fine, and there was no signal lower down towards Camp 2, it never occurred to us that the lads might be in trouble. We were certain they had descended lower, lost signal, had flat phones, and so on—but were descending. There was essentially no reason to think anything else. They werebujacos”—tough guys. They had survived a night without sleeping bags at 6,400m, and now they would just run down to us, to the girls, to lunch, to a beer.

Given these assumptions, we knew we had several hours of waiting before they would appear above Camp 1, which could be seen from the hut. Meanwhile, we tried to contact them by radio and by phone, in case they happened to come into signal. But still, we were completely calm.

When about 3 p.m. came and there was no sign of them, though, it seemed odd to us. It had gone on too long. We decided we would go up and look for them. They were probably tired and frozen, and another night up there would be truly critical.

We ate, brewed tea, and took all the clothing we had. In the meantime, I called a friend, a guide, down in town so that he knew something was going on and could start rounding up help if needed. But there was still a chance alive in us that they were at Camp 1, asleep in the tent, and simply had no way of letting us know. Down in town, a group of volunteers was already getting ready to set out to help. There is no organized professional mountain rescue in Peru.

But there was no one at Camp 1. By then we sensed that things were bad; we just didn’t know how bad. On top of that, a snowstorm was moving in.

The message we sent down was clear: It was official, we needed help. The lads were somewhere up there. We were going up for them. We knew that down in the valley things were already under way and would keep going without us. But we had a different task: to find them and bring them down.

We pack all their sleeping bags as well and set off up into the gale. Matěj created a Garmin messaging group and made our position trackable online. He realized more than I did that we were heading into real trouble.

I have friends up there – my “churaks,” my “hermanos.” I registered nothing.

For the first time in my life, I was faster than Matěj. In my head, I knew they were up there, frozen and exhausted but alive, and that we were their only chance.

In these conditions I would turn around on any climb of my own; I would never go on. But now it wasn’t about me. It was about the lads. The moment we gave up, they would have no chance.

We got as far as above Camp 2, to 6,000m, but there was nothing anywhere. That was shortly before midnight. Just gale, snowfall, a temperature I’d estimate at around minus 30 degrees, and not a trace of the lads.

By this point I knew things were very bad. In this weather, no one survives up here for even an hour. Matýsek was very afraid of going down because of the avalanche danger and pushed for us to stay at Camp 2 and somehow sit it out with all those sleeping bags. I knew that if we stayed, we would be dead within the hour. In the end, I convinced him that we had to go down. That was about 50/50. But staying up there felt to me like 90/10.

The descent was horrific. Avalanche terrain was everywhere we looked, with giant seracs over our heads almost the entire way. Visibility was near zero, and our tracks drifted over with snow. Nevertheless, our guardian angel was on duty again and guided us down. We get back to Camp 1 sometime around 3 a.m. on Wednesday, July 15. There I well and truly collapsed, mentally and physically. We crawled into the tent for a while and waited for the first group of volunteer rescuers, who, as we already knew, were on their way from the refugio.

Even in the tent and in a sleeping bag, 700 vertical meters lower, the weather was terrible, and it was here that Matýsek told me we were one–all. He had saved us above that crevasse, and I had by insisting that we go down.

The first rescue team reached Camp 1 just after first light, sometime after 6 a.m. It was five people in all, three guides and two aspirant guides, and among them my good friend Szymon, who was the only one closer to me than Saul. That was all. We handed over all the information we had and let them go up. We would head for the refugio, where further teams were supposedly bound: one to set up a support base at Camp 2, another at Camp 1. Supposedly lots of people were coming.

Unfortunately, we were in Peru, where everything does not quite work. We met the Camp 1 team below the glacier—five lads, all friends, climbers, and volunteers from Peru, Colombia, Spain, and the Czech Republic. No Camp 2 team ever came, and in fact, there never was one. The five lads up above had packed late in the evening and set out from Huaraz to help friends in need. In a single push they climbed to over 6,000m. But because of the lack of support and the conditions up there, they had to turn back in the afternoon and descend to Camp 1. That essentially ended any realistic chance of finding the lads alive. I personally had known already at Camp 2, which we had climbed to in our attempt to find them, that there was almost no chance left.

From then on, great chaos set in across the whole organization of the rescue and search. As I already mentioned, Peru has no mountain rescue service; there are no rescue helicopters, no rescuers, no system, no protocol, no designated person to take charge of any coordination. Everything happened through the WhatsApp group that Matěj created when we set out for the lads. Everything was done on the basis of volunteers, friends, guides and family members.

Eventually, the police, the high-mountain police (Policía de Alta Montaña), and apparently even the army joined in to help. But their help consisted solely of transporting volunteers by helicopter from the town of Caraz to the refugio at 4,650m, and the first helicopter took off 24 hours after the official call for help. What’s more, it flew up empty, to assess the situation, only to find that another police helicopter had been sitting on the “helipad” by the refugio for several days, badly parked, and that its pilots were afraid to take off in it.

In the end, some transport up did take place, but effectively only on the following days. If someone down below had been able to coordinate it, they could have sent the line of volunteers towards base camp on foot, with a herd of donkeys, on the very first day of the search (July 15), and they would have been there far sooner than waiting until the next day to see whether anyone would get a place on the helicopter.

The entire rescue, and subsequently the search, was run by volunteers and the families. It was they who hired private companies with advanced drones, they who coordinated everything, mainly with the police, they who did everything they could. I myself went back with two volunteers a few days later to a point just above Camp 1 to attempt a further search with drones—without success. The zone where all the clues ended—GPS signals, the location of the last photos, the hint of the last tracks, and so on—is at around 6,500m. Camp 1 is at 5,300m, and most drones simply cannot fly that high and that far. Only the advanced police drones can, and they in turn lacked experienced pilots willing to risk it. Going higher wasn’t possible because of the conditions on the mountain. So, a stalemate. In the end footage from the suspect zone was obtained, but there was not a trace of the lads.

After almost detective-like work by many friends—using the GPS signal from their watch as requested from Garmin headquarters, footage from private and police drones, plus footage from the guides who went all the way up during the search, information from us, and the lads’ last photos—it appears that the lads missed our belay station by literally a few meters, and that while crossing a snow bridge over gigantic crevasse systems it collapsed beneath them.

When I first wrote this report, it had already been more than three weeks since I last saw the lads, since I last spoke to them. Still nothing has been found today, and in my view it will be hard for anything to be found. The scale of everything on Huascarán is utterly absurd: crevasses several hundred meters wide and deep, seracs the size of apartment blocks… Sure, this isn’t the Himalaya, and it doesn’t look like it, either. Huascarán looks like two lazy fat blokes lying side by side. But beware of it. That mountain is mean and hostile, and although on paper it isn’t hard—the Normal Route was reportedly graded only AD/AD+, the Escudo D—it is no game. You are playing for your life at every single step.

I don’t think that either we or the lads made any fundamental mistake; it was simply that Matýsek and I had a large dose of luck, while the lads had a little less of it.

I am not writing this story for sensation. It is just one real story with a tragic ending. If at least a few of you take something from it—ideally some kind of lesson—it will have served its purpose. As we all know, the mountains give, but sometimes they also take away. We unfortunately now know that this is no empty phrase but the hard truth.

Editor’s note: This trip report has been translated from the original Czech into English with permission of the author. Place names and local terms (La Garganta, El Escudo, refugio, Huaracino, pollo a la brasa) have not been translated from the original Spanish.

These Are the Bestselling Electric Pickup Trucks

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

A total of 232,809 electric pickup trucks have sold since the start of 2024. Ford alone sells nearly that many gas-powered F-series pickups every single quarter.

Electric pickup trucks are still a relatively new idea, but they've now been along long enough to gather some data on how they're selling. We totaled sales numbers from 2024, 2025, and 2026 so far to see which electric pickup trucks have had the biggest impact on the market in the past few years.

For some perspective, electric pickups still make up a tiny portion of the overall truck market in the U.S. Even if you combine the sales of all six models listed here, over a time period of nearly three years, you get 232,809 units sold in total. Ford sold 801,525 units of the F-series pickup in 2025 alone. There are also aren’t any pickup trucks on our list of the bestselling EVs in 2026, a list which contains mostly SUVs.

The sales totals for each model listed here include figures from the full-year 2024, the full-year 2025, and through the third quarter of 2026. In cases where the automaker doesn't release detailed sales figures, we used estimates from Cox Automotive.

If you feel like you see Cybertrucks everywhere you go, that's not just because their distinctive styling makes them stand out on the road. Tesla has sold quite a few pickup trucks in the past few years, which may seem surprising given the Cybertruck's polarizing design and numerous recalls early in its production run. Tesla started selling the Cybertruck in 2024, and that was the strongest year of sales, suggesting that eager early adopters made up much of the early run of 38,965 units sold that year. Sales declined to around 20,000 units in 2025, and through the third quarter of 2026 Tesla has moved an estimated 11,586 units.

Ford was one of the first to enter the electric-truck market, and its strategy hinged on the F-150 Lightning's similarities to the top-selling F-150 pickup. The F-150 Lightning has since been discontinued, ending production in 2025, but it managed to rack up a significant number of sales. Keep in mind that the Lightning went on sale for the 2022 model year, well before many of the other models on this list, so the sales totals here reflect only the latter half of its production run.

General Motors' Hummer EV pickup was also early to the trend, debuting for the 2022 model year like the F-150 Lightning. But it took a completely different approach with its wild styling, mega-horsepower powertrain, and ridiculous off-road capability. GMC added an SUV sibling for 2024, and the caveat to the Hummer's sales numbers are that they include both body styles. We aren't able to break out numbers for just the pickup, because GM doesn't report those figures separately.

Chevy's electric version of the Silverado pickup got off to a slow start, racking up less than 8000 sales in 2024, its first year on the market. Those numbers picked up for 2025 as Chevrolet added lower-cost variants meant for fleets, cresting 11,000 units that year. But it appears demand has softened once again, as Chevy only moved 5327 units through the first three quarters of this year.

Rivian's debut model was the R1T, which was exactly as innovative as you would expect from a startup automaker. With impressive performance, futuristic styling, and new ideas such as a storage tunnel between the cab and the bed, the R1T aimed more at lifestyle buyers than at traditional pickup customers. The R1T went on sale for the 2022 model year, and sales have been on a steady decline since 2024, Rivian's recently launched compact R2 SUV is now outselling the larger R1 lineup, which makes us wonder if there is room for Rivian to add a pickup version of that model that would offer a lower cost of entry.

Riding on the same platform as the Chevrolet Silverado EV, the Sierra EV aims for a middle ground between GM's other electric pickups. It's more upscale than its Chevy sibling, especially in Denali trim, but far more conventional-looking than the other GMC EV truck, the wild Hummer EV. Its sales numbers followed a similar trend as the Chevy, with a slow start in 2024, a rise in 2025, and a drop again in 2026.

Despite being raised on a steady diet of base-model Hondas and Toyotas—or perhaps because of it—Joey Capparella cultivated an obsession for the automotive industry throughout his childhood in Nashville, Tennessee. He found a way to write about cars for the school newspaper during his college years at Rice University, which eventually led him to move to Ann Arbor, Michigan, for his first professional auto-writing gig at Automobile Magazine. He has been part of the Hearst Autos team since 2016 and now lives in New York City.

Ukraine’s drone strikes spur hasty Russian plans for underground data centers

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Russia tries to fix exposed digital infrastructure as data center war backfires.

Russian tech companies are scrambling to harden and hide their data centers underground after Ukrainian drone strikes damaged facilities housing AI supercomputers and disrupted cloud services for millions of Russian users. Meanwhile, the Kremlin is requiring data center owners to reserve computing capacity for emergency state needs and has even authorized possible use of foreign data centers.

These consequences of Russia’s war against Ukraine come as the Russian military has bombarded Ukrainian cities with drones and missiles nearly night and day in recent months, along with specifically targeting Ukrainian data centers since late September. But Ukraine has retaliated with its own drone strikes on military and infrastructure targets across Russia, degrading or knocking out data centers operated by Russian tech giant Yandex, the state-owned bank Sberbank, social media site VKontakte, and the Russian Ministry of Finance.

Now, state-owned telecommunications company Rostelcom has raised the possibility of moving some Russian data centers underground in talks with the nickel and palladium mining company Norilsk, according to Russian newspaper Vedomosti. The industry publication Data Center Dynamics also pointed to a recent proposal by a Russian regional governor to build data centers within open-pit coal mines while planting green public parks above as camouflage.

On November 2, Putin also signed a decree ordering Russian data center operators to set aside 10 percent of available space and capacity in case the Russian government requires it, according to independent Russian news publication Meduza. That may indicate growing Kremlin concerns about the vulnerability of Russian cloud computing infrastructure during its war with Ukraine.

Putin’s decree also allows a special Russian government commission to authorize use of foreign data centers in consultation with Russia’s Federal Security Service. That marks a notable policy change because Putin and the Russian parliament previously enacted Russia’s “Sovereign Internet Law,” which has blocked Russian companies from using cloud computing infrastructure outside Russia’s borders.

Russia’s push for a sovereign and increasingly closed Internet has resulted in a highly centralized system under a few Russian tech companies like Yandex. That hyper-centralized system has now proven vulnerable during wartime, as Ukrainian drone strikes on key Russian data centers created widespread disruptions for popular payment and digital banking apps, e-commerce platforms, video streaming services, and ride-hailing and food delivery apps, among others.

Yandex has also been forced to relocate all three of its main supercomputers after Ukrainian drone strikes targeted the AI data centers where they were originally housed, Meduza reported.

By comparison, Ukraine’s decentralized digital services have proven much more resilient despite years of Russian drone and missile attacks, especially as the government has taken more steps to disperse crucial energy and telecommunications nodes and bury more supporting infrastructure underground.

A big boost came from US tech giants Amazon and Microsoft, which helped Ukraine migrate government resources and other crucial digital services to cloud regions elsewhere in Europe shortly after Russia launched its full-scale invasion in February 2022. The Ukrainian government has also used SpaceX’s Starlink satellite terminals—paid for by the United States and European countries such as Poland and Germany—as a backup communications system.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Amazon announces $25 “complete” Thanksgiving meal with same-day delivery

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Amazon is getting into the Thanksgiving dinner game with a surprisingly affordable $25 holiday meal that includes a turkey, sides, dessert, and even same-day delivery.

With Thanksgiving just weeks away, retailers are rolling out deals to make feeding the family a little easier on the wallet.

Now, Amazon has announced its own Thanksgiving meal deal, offering an entire holiday spread for just $25 through Amazon Grocery.

The promotion is available in more than 2,300 US cities and towns, with eligible customers able to have their groceries delivered within hours.

The centerpiece of Amazon’s Thanksgiving bundle is an eight-pound frozen Butterball turkey, accompanied by a surprisingly extensive selection of sides.

Customers will receive Ocean Spray cranberry sauce, two packets of McCormick turkey gravy, two boxes of Kraft Mac & Cheese, Stove Top stuffing, and ingredients for green bean casserole.

There’s also five pounds of russet potatoes, carrots, corn, crescent rolls, and everything from pumpkin puree to pie crusts for dessert.

Of course, there’s a catch to Amazon’s definition of a “complete” meal. Unlike a fully prepared Thanksgiving dinner, customers will need to cook everything themselves, and certain pantry staples may need to be purchased separately.

The deal comes just days after Costco unveiled its massive 30-pound Thanksgiving dinner for $200, which includes enough food to feed eight people.

Amazon is offering additional Thanksgiving savings throughout November, including frozen turkeys for as little as $0.79 per pound and fresh turkeys starting at $0.99 per pound.

Shoppers can also save 40% on premade sides between November 15 and November 26, potentially making things easier for anyone who doesn’t want to spend the holiday cooking.

Amazon’s $25 Thanksgiving meal is available to order online through November 26, while supplies last. The company is advertising free same-day delivery on qualifying orders of $25 or more in eligible areas.

Pennsylvania is way ahead on NEVI charging – now comes another $37M

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Pennsylvania is putting $37 million in federal NEVI funding toward what PennDOT says will be 515 new public EV charging ports across the Philadelphia region, including curbside chargers.

The awards cover Bucks, Chester, Delaware, Montgomery, and Philadelphia counties. PennDOT says the projects will expand public charging availability by 27% across the region and 53% in Philadelphia.

For drivers without a driveway, curbside charging gives them somewhere to plug in while parked near home.

It’s Electric PA received $2.93 million for 157 on-street Level 2 ports across 13 Philadelphia neighborhoods. Voltpost received funding for another 62 curbside ports across the four suburban counties.

Fast charging is getting funding, too. PositivEnergy received $7.2 million for 54 DC fast-charging ports at 11 Philadelphia Parking Authority lots. Tesla received two $392,000 awards for eight ports each at Royal Farms in Broomall and Wawa in Plymouth Meeting.

The money comes from the federal National Electric Vehicle Infrastructure (NEVI) program, created by the Biden administration’s Bipartisan Infrastructure Law. Pennsylvania’s five-year allocation is $171.5 million.

NEVI initially focused on fast charging along designated highway corridors. States can spend the money more broadly after the Federal Highway Administration certifies that their corridor charging requirements have been met. Pennsylvania received that certification on September 22, 2025.

In fact, Pennsylvania has built 54 EV charging stations using NEVI funds so far – more than double the number of any other state.

PennDOT’s community charging program supports both Level 2 and DC fast charging. Its published program minimum requirements include at least four ports per proposed station, at least 6 kW per port, two ADA-compliant parking spaces, and J1772 or CCS connectors on every port. Equipment must also meet Buy America requirements.

PennDOT lists 71 awards in this southeastern round as part of its roughly $100 million statewide community charging effort.

Applications for the western region close November 20. Central and eastern Pennsylvania applications close March 12, 2027.

For the southeastern projects, PennDOT’s estimated schedule puts environmental reviews in November 2026 through January 2027, followed by agreement execution in January and February. Drivers will still have to wait for construction and commissioning before these awards become places to plug in.

French Museum’s ‘Wanted’ Poster Campaign for Stolen Art Leads to Return of Missing 17th-Century Dutch Painting

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Thanks to an inventive “Wanted” campaign by the Musée des Beaux-Arts d’Orléans in France, aimed at tracking down hundreds of WWII-era looted artworks, one painting has just been found, the museum’s director told ARTnews.

A 17th-century painting by Dutch artist Willem Cornelisz Duyster, titled Un Buveur (The Drinker) and stolen in 1940 from the Orléans museum, was located last month at an auction house in Switzerland and is on its way back home to the French museum, Le Journal de l’Art reported earlier. Related Articles Italy Looks to Join Its European Counterparts With Restitution Legislation Swiss Government to Fund Research into Holdings of Cultural Artifacts

After being presented with the evidence of the theft, “the possessor of the work immediately agreed to restitute it at no cost,” said Olivia Voisin, the museum’s director. The painting is currently being held in customs at the French border. Voisin said the museum was able to find the missing painting thanks to a recent campaign that uses a bit of humor to spread awareness about some 350 artworks that were plundered from the Musée Paul Fourché, an annex of the Beaux-Arts museum, after the city was attacked by Nazis in June 1940. It is believed people on both sides of the conflict took advantage of the opportunity to ransack the museum.

The poster campaign features the words “WANTED” written in chunky, all caps in American Old Western font, across a selection of black-and-white images of the museum’s lost artworks. These have been shared on social media, the museum website, and other news platforms, and are bolstered by a two-year-old digital catalog of the stolen works.

So far, it seems to be working. One local from Orléans, who saw the “Wanted” posters, decided to spend his free time searching for the missing paintings. “He wanted to help us, and that was the goal of the campaign: that these searches are shared or collective,” said Voisin.

While randomly sifting through online auctions, the unnamed Orléans resident came across Un Buveur in a catalog for an October 17 sale at the Gloggner auction house in Lucerne. It was estimated to be worth about $3,400 and thought to be a study of the original. It depicts a jolly scene of a seated man, dressed in contemporary garb and holding out an empty glass; he raises his other arm and appears to be caught in midsentence.

The museum was tipped off about the curious painting and contacted the auction house, which immediately cooperated and “was very transparent throughout,” said Voisin, noting that attitudes have changed in that regard. After she presented the painting’s holder with an image of the original superimposed on a digital image of their painting, they saw it was a perfect match. He had purchased it from a London auction house in the 1990s.

“He immediately proposed to restitute the painting,” she said. “It was pretty natural for him to return it, because he felt that he had been able to enjoy it for 35 years, and it was time for it to go home.”

The painting is considered one of Duyster’s finest, and in many ways represents the museum’s stolen collection, which once held several similar Dutch paintings from the 17th century showing scenes of revelry. However, the painting appears to be damaged by several layers of overpainting and cleaning attempts. If in good condition, Voisin estimated it to be worth between €25,000 and €30,000 ($28,000 to $33,600).

The Orléans museum has been trying for years to recover the artworks lost during World War II, with little luck. To date, 424 paintings are still missing from its collection, counting additional paintings that have gone missing or were stolen at different periods. They include works by Édouard Manet, Hans Holbein the Younger, Anthony van Dyck, Lorenzo Costa, and one attributed to Raphael’s Renaissance workshop. Since the creation of the digital catalog and the “Wanted” poster campaign, four paintings, including Un Buveur, have been returned over the past few years.

Justin Sun Accuses Trump-Backed World Liberty of Using UAE Ties to Escape Federal Court

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Justin Sun Accuses Trump-Backed World Liberty of Using UAE Ties to Escape Federal Court

Justin Sun’s legal fight with World Liberty Financial now hinges on a question of nationality.

The Tron founder said Tuesday on X that his lawyers have asked a federal court in California to sanction the Trump family-backed crypto venture over how long it took to disclose that its ownership chain gives it citizenship in the United Arab Emirates.

Sun says he is one of World Liberty’s earliest and largest investors. He agreed in November 2024 to buy $30 million of its WLFI tokens and later raised his total investment to $45 million.

In April, he sued the company, alleging it misled him into investing and then unlawfully froze his tokens. World Liberty has fired back in court, alleging that Sun shorted WLFI and drove down its price.

The current dispute turns on a technical point. Sun chose to sue in federal court, which can hear cases between parties from different places but generally can’t hear a foreign plaintiff’s case against a foreign defendant.

As Sun explained it, a company like World Liberty takes on the citizenship of each of its owners, so a single foreign owner makes the whole company foreign for those purposes.

According to Sun, World Liberty’s initial court disclosure listed only itself, with Florida as its sole citizenship. A judge signaled at an August hearing that some of Sun’s claims should stay in open court rather than go to arbitration.

Less than a week later, Sun says, the company amended the disclosure to list 22 members in its ownership chain, with citizenship in Florida, Delaware, Puerto Rico, and the UAE. World Liberty now argues that the federal court lacks jurisdiction over the case, according to Sun.

Sun argues the late disclosure was a tactic to move the case out of federal court, and his motion seeks to make World Liberty and its lawyers bear the cost. He contrasted the company’s position with its own marketing, posting excerpts from World Liberty’s “gold paper” that describe it as “an American, tax-paying, corporation.”

Once the sanctions motion is resolved, Sun said, he will refile his lawsuit in California state court. “I will keep fighting for the transparency this community deserves,” he wrote.

The feud has played out publicly for months. Sun has criticized how World Liberty treats its investors and alleged that the company threatened to burn his WLFI tokens.

Former High Museum COO Sentenced to One Year in Prison for Siphoning $600,000 to Buy Guitars

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

A federal judge sentenced the former chief operating officer of the High Museum of Art in Atlanta to one year in prison and one year of supervised release for stealing more than $600,000 from the museum.

Brady Lum, 60, served as the High Museum’s COO from January 2019 until December 2025, when he resigned. He began siphoning money from the museum in September 2019, around nine months into the job, until November 2025, when it was discovered.

Lum’s theft involved the purchase of “non-business items and services for himself, including expensive guitars and other music equipment, personal music lessons, and woodworking equipment, through direct supplier invoicing and the High Museum’s corporate credit card reimbursement process,” according to a press release from the Office of US Attorney for the Northern District of Georgia, which prosecuted the case. Related Articles High Museum of Art's Former COO Pleads Guilty to Embezzling More Than $600,000 $50,000 Driskell Prize Goes to Cheryl Finley of Spelman College

The release adds that Lum concealed the theft by “submitting altered invoices, using his position to approve expenses, and using accounting adjustments to spread his expenses across different cost centers.”

“Lum brazenly pilfered hundreds of thousands of dollars from a beloved civic institution to pay for his high-end guitars and other personal items. His appalling breach of trust damaged not only the High Museum but also the larger art community,” US Attorney Theodore S. Hertzberg said in a statement.

Prior to the sentencing on Monday, Lum had paid in full the $607,688.18 he had taken as part of his plea deal, which was announced in July. He also voluntarily paid the High Museum $500,000 to cover its investigative expenses and legal fees.

“Lum repaid the High Museum by covering its losses and expenses, and now he will pay his debt to society from prison,” Hertzberg added.

A request for comment from the High Museum was not immediately returned.

Google will disable Search for students unless schools accept AI features in ‘Guided Learning’ push

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Google is today launching new AI settings for schools, but with the big catch that Search is fully disabled if AI features are turned off entirely.

In a blog post today, Google says it is “focused on building technology responsibly to help students thrive” and, as such, will tailor its AI efforts to help students. “Guided Learning” in Gemini, Search, and Classroom will include helping students “work through problems without skipping straight to answers.”

…if a student pastes a complex physics problem or asks for help writing a book report in Search, they’ll see prompts to help them think critically and work through the concepts, without going directly to the answer.

That’s launching in a pilot program this month in select US schools, with Guided Learning in Google Classroom launching globally today. Google describes this as a “1-1 study tool, anchored in class material to help [students] stay on task” while also giving the teacher insights into how their class is handling the material.

Beyond this new Guided Learning push, Google also says that it will now turn off “Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search” for K-12 student accounts by default “until school administrators explicitly turn them on.”

As The New York Times points out, having those features fully off has a big side effect: it cuts students off from Google Search entirely.

Google says that this is because Search and AI “are deeply integrated” and confirms that students in schools with AI features turned off (which, to reiterate, is now the default setting) “will not have access to search.”

As a reminder, Google Search’s AI features can largely be turned off using a simple modifier.

Google’s post says that the “goal is simple: provide school leaders the control and confidence to use AI products in classrooms as they see fit.” Yet, speaking to The New York Times, Google further explained that two options will be available to schools in “early 2027,” one that has AI Overviews and other AI features turned on, the other with Guided Learning in their place, leaving schools with no choice other than to accept AI features or give up access to Google Search for their students entirely.

NYT points out repeatedly that Google’s prior AI pushes in schools have been met with fierce opposition from educators and parents alike.

STAT+: Amid Mass General Brigham layoffs, CEO pens vague letter, stirring staff anxiety

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

By Jonathan Saltzman and Marin Wolf — Boston Globe

Less than two weeks after Mass General Brigham said it had begun laying off workers, the head of the health care system has sent employees a cryptic email saying MGB faces urgent financial challenges and needed to make improvements but provided no specifics.

The letter emailed late Monday by Dr. Anne Klibanski, chief executive of the state’s largest health system, said MGB is changing how it operates and uses its workforce. But it provided no details, never mentioned layoffs, and raised more questions than it answered.

“We must improve our performance and make thoughtful decisions about how we use our resources,” Klibanski said in the letter sent late Monday to its roughly 85,000 employees.

Deep inside a Spanish cave, archaeologists found at least 120 Latin inscriptions, and five of them honor a goddess called ‘the Lady’

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

At least 120 Latin inscriptions in Cova Dones, Spain, name a goddess called Domina, the Lady, and point to women taking an active part in her cult.

One chamber of Cova Dones, a cave in Millares in Valencia province, now holds at least 120 Latin inscriptions. Teams from the universities of Alicante and Zaragoza recorded them during fieldwork in May and June 2026.

When the room was first explored in 2024, about 15 had been logged.

Five of the texts are vows to Domina, “the Lady,” a goddess the visitors addressed with reverence. Several of the names read so far are women’s. The team calls the results preliminary, and many texts are still being read.

The researchers call the room the Roman Sanctuary. It sits more than 650 feet (200 meters) from the entrance, according to the University of Alicante.

Most of the texts are believed to date to the 1st and 2nd centuries AD, and they cover the walls and the ceiling. Small natural pools on the floor, known as gours, catch water from the galleries, and visitors dropped offerings into them.

That first campaign also yielded a coin from the reign of Emperor Claudius. Someone had wedged it as an offering between a fissure and a stalactite in the ceiling, and in a team photo Xabier Garín Artázcoz points at the crack. Trinidad Pasíes Oviedo of the Valencia Museum of Prehistory recovered and conserved the coin.

The January 2025 announcement from the University of Alicante placed the room among the deepest known Roman inscription sites in Iberia, second only to Cueva del Puente in Burgos and possibly Cueva de Román de Clunia.

This year’s readings were done by two epigraphy specialists. Silvia Alfayé teaches ancient history at the University of Zaragoza, and Sergio García-Dils lectures at UNED in Seville. García-Dils also directs the Spanish School of Speleology.

According to Alfayé, five votive texts show that women “played an active role in her cult.” Three of them carry the word MALACI. In a release dated July 27, the University of Alicante called it an unpublished epithet for a goddess not known until now.

García-Dils noted that the texts are packed into one marked-off part of the cave, well away from the entrance. To him that “strongly suggests the existence of a well-organised and structured cult” with a named deity, not scattered visits.

The University of Southampton, where Aitor Ruiz-Redondo is also based, put out the English release on October 20. It says early readings suggest some women wrote their vows themselves, without a go-between.

Until now, the Roman cave sanctuary with the largest known set of inscriptions was La Griega, with 107. One of them names a woman.

The 2026 team logged objects on the chamber floor, among them two bronze rings found under the inscribed rock. There were two bronze rings, a bracelet, chalice-shaped cups, spindle whorls, bits of pottery and animal bones.

The whorls came up in numbers. Virginia Barciela co-leads the project with Ruiz-Redondo and teaches prehistory at Alicante. She said the whorls probably point to offerings of spindles, a textile tool tied to women in antiquity.

She also brought up the first written name of the site, Cova de les Dones, meaning Cave of the Women. That name may hold “the fossilised memory of a place long associated with women,” she said, or with female spirits.

Alfayé said the goddess’s title may have to do with water. Water shrines existed elsewhere in the ancient world, such as a circular water shrine at Pelusium in northern Sinai.

The inscriptions are extremely difficult to see with the naked eye. So the team photographed the surfaces for high-resolution photogrammetric models and read the texts on those.

That work turned up inscriptions no one had seen. It also split apart lines that earlier readers had taken as one text.

Jorge Angás and Carlos Valladares, from the IPH institute in Zaragoza and the DigHER project, ran the geomatics side. Javier Velaza Frías, a Latin professor in Barcelona, leads the study of the Roman inscriptions. Víctor Sabaté Vidal of the University of Valencia has also contributed to the project.

Latin wishes turn up on other Roman objects too, for instance openwork glass cups carved with drinking wishes in Greek or Latin.

In a Roman underground sanctuary, finding texts and offerings together in place is exceptionally rare, says the Southampton release.

Ruiz-Redondo, Barciela and archaeologist Ximo Martorell published the first results in 2023. In the journal Antiquity they described over 100 Paleolithic paintings and engravings. Some are older than 24,000 years.

Pottery from the Iron Age had already led archaeologists in the 1960s to class it as an Iberian sanctuary. In 2025 the team logged more than 100 stalagmites that people had modified on purpose.

For that kind of find, the University of Alicante ranks the cave second in the world, after Saint-Marcel in France.

Ruiz-Redondo said people may have kept returning for “its permanent water supply,” its landscape or its easy access.

At La Pasiega, Maltravieso and Ardales, red marks have been dated to more than 64,000 years ago.

Alfayé and her colleagues are still working on the goddess’s title. For now the team has not said who Domina was or what MALACI means. Many inscriptions are still being read, and much of the cave is unstudied, so 120 is a minimum.

The DONARQ project started in 2023 with money from the Generalitat Valenciana and the British Academy. In September 2026 the sanctuary was shortlisted for a National Geographic +Historia readers’ prize. Public voting closed on October 12.

This year the regional Pla Restaura program and the Millares town council are paying for more work. The money goes to the cave’s access and entrance, safety measures, a digital twin and a catalog of the Paleolithic images. The company Arquivolta Patrimoni i Societat is coordinating it, and virtual visits should follow.

Among those offerings, two bronze rings sat on the surface right below the inscribed rock.

The information was obtained from the University of Southampton , the University of Alicante and the University of Zaragoza.

The Ford Bronco Frostwarden Makes 1200 HP, Thanks to Its Megazilla 2.0 Supercharged V-8

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

This search-and-rescue machine, revealed at SEMA, is what happens when Ford throws everything it has at a Bronco.

The folks at Ford brought one of the wildest Broncos we’ve ever seen to the 2026 SEMA Show this year. It’s called “Frostwarden” ... and it has 1200 horsepower.

How’d Ford extract that much horsepower from a Bronco? Well, it sure doesn’t use any version of the production engines. Instead, Ford put the also-new Megazilla 2.0 crate motor under the hood. This 7.3-liter V-8 is paired here with a 3.0-liter Gen 6 Whipple supercharger; it also has a stronger block, supercharger-specific camshaft, upgraded pistons, larger fuel injectors, and a different belt drive and damper. As a package, Ford is claiming 1200 hp and 1000 pound-feet of torque when run with race fuel. (No, it didn’t quote a pump gas number.)

The idea behind the rest of the rig was to put together a package that could be used as an arctic search-and-rescue machine. To that end, it has a custom Bilstein suspension, 74Weld portal gear lifts, upgraded Dana driveline, and 18-inch Weld bead-lock wheels wrapped in 42-inch BFGoodrich tires. It’s absolutely enormous, and gives off the impression that one could drive it anywhere, which is largely the point.

This Bronco is festooned with accessories front to back. Rigid lights should help show the way through snowstorms. A spare wheels/tire and pair of toolboxes sit atop the roof. There’s a shovel, recovery rope, fuel jugs, and more. Plus, Ford outfitted the interior with color-matching seats and accents. There’s even a “Megazilla” emblem in the steering wheel rim to make sure you don’t forget this beast has 1200 horsepower ready to unleash.

Unfortunately, Ford explicitly says these vehicle “is not available for purchase,” and we suspect something like it never will be from your local Ford dealer. However, it serves as proof that you can shoehorn a 7.3-liter supercharged V-8 under the (admittedly bulged) hood of a Bronco, and what’s not to love about that?

Ebenezer’s Venmo and ‘How to Rob a Bank’ ATMs: Studios Are Now Promoting Movies by Just Giving Away Money

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Clipfarming, influencer screenings, and meme-bait popcorn buckets: promoting a movie in the internet age is often a full-court offensive to get noticed. In that spirit, why not just get down to business and resort to modern civilization’s oldest method for seeking attention: dangling some money in people’s faces?

That’s what the marketing teams behind “How to Rob a Bank,” from Amazon MGM Studios, and “Ebenezer,” from Skydance’s Paramount Pictures, have tried in the weeks leading up to their shared November 13 release to North American theaters.

For the former ensemble heist thriller, a branded ATM was moved between multiplexes throughout the Los Angeles area over the course of four days. Those who learned of the campaign on the film‘s social media channels could gain clues about the machine’s location and a specified PIN number, which would spark payouts of a single one-hundred-dollar bill to those who bothered to seek out (or stumble upon) it. Related Stories What Are Local Movie Theaters Showing on Election Night? Don’t Forget, Oscar Watchers: ‘The Odyssey,’ ‘Project Hail Mary,’ and Other Big Guns Are Coming Back

“Ebenezer” took a more cheeky, trifling approach, promoting an official Venmo account for Johnny Depp’s Christmas crab apple that users could request funds from. Those who scrolled through the account’s transactions would see it shares the character’s penny-pinching spirit: the only completed transactions were those giving away one single cent. The Venmo account was only active for a few hours, though a Paramount Instagram post from last week teases followers to “check back” as “Ebenezer might be feeling generous on @venmo again.”

Several film news aggregation accounts like Culture Crave and DiscussingFilm reported that the account “crashed” due an overwhelming amount of requests, though Paramount declined to comment on if such a description was accurate. Venmo did not respond to a request asking if the company partnered with the studio on the sponsorship.

Much like that line, there is also a tackiness du jour to both money-mindset stunts, especially in a world where awards shows, professional sports and geopolitical events can hardly be watched without outlets asking you to bet — erh, predict — on them. A video posted to Paramount’s socials opens with a Venmo request for $500, asking Scrooge to help pay off a user’s student loans.

Not to mention the real inadvertent quirk: the account has been able to receive payments from users. Considering the scant number of one-cent payouts from the account and the number of influxes, it is almost certain that Paramount could have made money on the stunt, though an individual with knowledge of the account shared that all received funds were sent back to users via private transactions.

Krystyna Ursta, a 27-year-old actress, video editor, and Depp fan in Los Angeles, shared that she gave about $30 to the account and received a full refund a few days later — plus an extra penny and an invitation to a special screening of the film in New York on Monday. She will be attending a local press screening instead. Ursta also posted a video of herself reacting to the account, one of many fan responses that flooded in on social media.

“I kind of figured that they will probably return it,” Ursta wrote over text after being reached through her Instagram handle, which she included in her Venmo transactions. “I’m such a big fan. I was happy to support the film.”

The rather gimmick-less gimmick of waving around money also helps capture the attention of people that aren’t necessarily interested in seeing the movie, but are going through the average modern experience of thinking about one’s financial situation. Aggregation accounts were quick to post graphics reporting on both stunts, with captions like “HOW TO ROB A BANK ATM’S ARE BEING SET UP AROUND LOS ANGELES AND GIVING PEOPLE FREE MONEY AND IT COULDN’T HAPPEN AT A BETTER TIME” and “EBENEZER SCROOGE WILL SEND YOU REAL MONEY ON VENMO BUT THERE’S A CATCH SWIPE FOR MORE.”

There’s also people like Scott, a 35-year-old based out of Atlanta that couldn’t help but engage with the “Ebenezer” Venmo account after seeing it on X. He sent a penny, then received one in return. (A disclaimer: six Venmo users were reached for interview about their Ebenezer experience by sending them $0.01 with this reporter’s contact information in the caption; Scott was the only response that timed out before publication.)

“Seeing Ebenezer is now on Venmo was too funny and stupid to not engage with,” he wrote over text. “There’s something very boring dystopia about it: the idea of a fictional character from a 200-year-old Christmas story being commercialized on Venmo just shows how dumb Hollywood can. … It’s like McDonald’s Monopoly: take our slop and we might give your money.”

Also helpful for the studios is how little dangling the potential of money actually costs. Sans staffing fees, producing the “Ebenezer” Venmo account and its activity used a sum less than the coins between the couch cushions on the Paramount lot. Per the “How to Rob a Bank” promo’s website, each location’s prize pool gave away ten one-hundred-dollar bills and twenty twenty-dollar-bills ($1,400 total), plus some theater-supplied prizes like gift cards. An employee at the Regal Sherman Oaks that worked on the day of the on-site promotion said the ATM seemed to deal out the rewards in a random order, as opposed to $100 dollar bills for the first finders. Amazon MGM declined to comment, while Stellar Agency Inc., which sponsored and administered the promotion, did not respond to a request.

Both had much smaller overheads than a “Now You See Me: Now You Don’t” promotion than ran in April 2025. To launch the magician threequel’s first trailer, Lionsgate reportedly gave away roughly $250,000 to those that texted a mysterious phone number displaying in Times Square in the hours up to the drop. Like “Ebenezer” and “How to Rob a Bank,” it was a promotion that sparked a viral moment with plenty of user-generated content, each sharing the widely desired fantasy of receiving free money.

Neon handed out basic resources for an “I Love Boosters” promotion in May, where stars KeKe Palmer and LaKeith Stanfield took over a Shell gas station in West Pico to give out complimentary refills to the first 70 cars that arrived. The stunt played on then-peaking fuel prices, which had skyrocketed in the wake of the U.S.’ initial bombing of Iran in February and the subsequent blockade of the Strait of Hormuz.

In the months since, President Trump has promised $5,000 checks to every adult U.S. citizen if the Republican Party retains control of Congress in Tuesday’s midterm elections. Trump announced the idea, his approval rating was at a dismal 35 percent, per a CNN aggregate of various polls. Though the offer is unprecedented and dubious on its face, it has been covered extensively — and it cost nothing to float it.

Five Creative Ways to Spend Your FSA Funds Before the End of the Year

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

If you have a flexible spending account (FSA), you know the drill: Use the funds before the end of the year, or lose them. Unfortunately, chances are slim you can pack the rest of the year with doctor's appointments. Does that mean your hard-earned funds go to waste? Definitely not. You can spend your remaining FSA funds on other qualifying healthcare costs. And what counts as "qualifying" might surprise you—for instance, you can use your FSA or HSA funds to get yourself a sweet new fitness tracker or smartwatch.

As the end of the year approaches, here are all the ways you can spend any leftover money in your FSA balance.

Many over-the-counter medications and health items are FSA-eligible, including pain relievers, cold medicine, bandages, dental care products, menstrual products, and more. Scan your medicine cabinet and make a list of anything you need to stock up on.

FSA/HSA eligibility for smartwatches and other fitness wearables is real, but the main “catch" (as I’ve previously explained) is proving the device is in fact necessary, which requires you to obtain a Letter of Medical Necessity (LMN).

Screenless recovery trackers like Whoop, Amazfit Helio Strap, and the Oura Ring can usually be justified as HSA/FSA eligible around sleep quality, HRV-based stress monitoring, or recovery tracking tied to a chronic condition. Similarly, smartwatches can qualify the same way a basic tracker does, as long as you can link a relevant documented health concern. For instance, Coros offers FSA/HSA eligibility for all its watches and heart rate monitors through its partnership with Truemed. Amazfit similarly lets you filter by HSA/FSA eligibility for its smartwatches.

If you want to get creative with it, the official FSA store lets you filter by "surprisingly eligible." Similar to the section above, for some of these items, you'll want to brush up on how exactly to get an LMN to justify certain tech as FSA-eligible for you personally. In the meantime, go crazy and snag yourself Epsom salt for $8.99, Supergoop! sunscreen for $38.00, or perhaps a snazzy massage gun for $349.99.

Knee braces, wrist splints, compression socks, and other orthopedic items can help relieve chronic pain issues. Rather than wait for these issues to rear their heads down the line, use FSA money to offset the costs of these fixes now. If you use eyeglasses or contacts, consider using your FSA dollars to update or refresh your prescriptions. Don't forget to stock up on eye drops and lens solution, too.

If you have medical procedures scheduled for early next year, many healthcare providers will allow you to pre-pay the estimated costs this calendar year using FSA funds. This includes dental work, physical therapy, chiropractic visits, Lasik eye surgery, and more.

However you choose to spend your FSA funds, don’t let that money go to waste. After all, it came out of your paycheck. Check in with your employer’s rules for any balance remaining on Dec. 31 this year, so you aren’t left forfeiting your hard-earned funds. You can find a complete list at the FSA store here.
